## Demo 11 — Full semantics

**The claim**: three OWL profiles, federation, and a storage split — with the console honest about which one answered and how completely.

**What you can show**: load an ontology and watch the profile badge resolve to EL rather than RL, with the reasoner that will run named next to it. Load one in *no* profile and watch reasoning refuse, naming the offending axiom. Override it and watch the result come back **marked partial**. Then run a federated query and see each remote row attributed to the endpoint that produced it — and a `SILENT` failure appear *in the result* rather than only in a log.

### Epic 98 / 99 — OWL EL and QL reasoning
- [x] EL and QL reasoners alongside RL — **Both shipped, 5 August 2026.** QL rewrites a SPARQL query's algebra against `rdfs:subClassOf` axioms into a `UNION` (new `graph-owl-reasoning-ql` crate, pure logic, no I/O) and hands it back to the same planner every query uses — nothing is materialised, proven by asserting the reasoning overlay stays empty. Authorization survives the rewrite for free: the rewrite runs *before* `scoped_facts` compiles the access predicate, so every `UNION` branch it adds is subject to the identical predicate a direct query would face — proven with two principals, not assumed. A construct QL cannot express (`owl:hasKey`, property chains, `owl:TransitiveProperty`/`FunctionalProperty`/`InverseFunctionalProperty`) is named in the response, not silently dropped. A rewrite deeper or wider than its budget reports truncation rather than completing a narrower walk and calling it whole. EL classifies via a `whelk` sidecar process (new `graph-owl-reasoning-el` crate) — `whelk` depends on LGPL-3.0 `horned-owl`, so it is invoked as a separate binary over a temp file and stdout, never linked (`cargo deny` never sees either crate). Measured for real, not assumed: 100,000 classes, 812,619 derived subsumptions, 2.77s against a 60s budget — found and fixed a genuine pipe-buffer deadlock along the way (a poll loop that never drained the child's piped stdout hung once output exceeded the OS buffer, invisible on every small test fixture). An axiom outside EL (`owl:allValuesFrom`, cardinality, `owl:unionOf`, `owl:complementOf`, `owl:inverseOf` — verified against the W3C spec's actual grammar, not summarised) is named, not silently classified around; explanations ("A ⊑ B ⊑ C ⊑ D") are re-derived locally since `whelk` returns only the flat final pairs; classification is cached by the TBox's own transaction-time watermark, invalidated by an ontology write and never by a data write. **Found while implementing**: neither QL nor RL nor EL reasons over the catalog's own `AssetKind` hierarchy — `Asset`'s `dsc:type` is a string property, not an `rdf:type` reference, so all three engines need a real ontology assertion (a SPARQL `INSERT`, an Epic 33 pack) independent of the containment system `AssetKind::parent_kind` already governs; see `99-owl-ql-reasoning.md`'s own note.
- [x] **`qlRewrite`/`refusedAxioms` now actually reach `/sparql`'s response — found and fixed 8 August 2026** (`plans/EPIC-COMPLETION-PLAN.md` Phase 1.2). Both fields existed on `SparqlOutcome` and were populated correctly server-side since this epic shipped, but `query_outcome_json` (the hand-built response the HTTP endpoint actually returns) omitted both — two checked acceptance criteria ("the rewritten query is retrievable", "an axiom outside QL is reported, not silently dropped") were true only inside the Rust process, never on the wire, because every RED test for this epic called `Catalog::sparql` directly rather than the HTTP layer. Fixed by serializing both fields (`ForbiddenConstruct` rendered by hand to a stable camelCase string, matching `RuleName`'s own convention, since it holds no `Sid` and gets no `Serialize` derive), proven by a new HTTP-level test in `crates/graph-owl-server/tests/sparql_ql_rewrite.rs`. Writing that test surfaced its own real gotcha, not a product bug: `Catalog::sparql`'s row-scoping (`scoped_facts`) narrows every SPARQL result to subjects that resolve through `list_assets_under_fqn` — a real, catalog-known asset — unlike SHACL validation's direct graph read. A synthetic `dsc:` subject asserted straight into the graph is invisible to `/sparql` no matter what it types itself as, silently producing `factsScanned: 0` with no error.
- [x] **No UI of their own — by design, verified 8 August 2026.** Which profile ran is a routing question, surfaced entirely by Epic 100 (`profile badge + reasoner attribution`, its own still-open item), not duplicated here — the same "the design working" pattern Epic 34 already established for entity families absorbed into the composable entity page. Neither `98-owl-el-reasoning.md` nor `99-owl-ql-reasoning.md` names a console/UI acceptance criterion anywhere in its own text, confirming this was never a planned surface deferred, only a correctly-scoped absence
- [x] **Epic 98's EL classification is now actually reachable — found and fixed 8 August 2026** (`plans/EPIC-COMPLETION-PLAN.md` Phase 1.3). `Catalog::classify_ontology`/`explain_subsumption` were correct, tested, and had no HTTP route at all — the sidecar invocation, budget handling, caching and local re-derivation were all real, but nothing in a running deployment could ever reach them, and nothing ever called `Catalog::with_el_sidecar` outside a test either, so even a deployed route would have had no sidecar configured. Fixed with two new routes (`POST /reasoning/el/classify`, `GET /reasoning/el/explain`, both following `/reasoning/runs`/`/reasoning/explain`'s existing shape) plus an opt-in `GRAPH_OWL_EL_SIDECAR` environment variable in `main.rs`, off by default like every other deployment-level capability here. Proven end to end in `crates/graph-owl-server/tests/reasoning_el.rs` — including the honest case this environment is actually in: no `whelk` binary installed, so the test proves the *named* `Validation` error a real deployment without one configured gets back, not a generic failure.

### Epic 100 — Profile detection & routing
- [x] **Detection across RL, EL, QL; incomparable profiles not reported as supersets** *(5 August 2026)*. New `graph-owl-ontology::profile` module: `detect_rl`/`detect_el`/`detect_ql` each check construct presence against that profile's own grammar — verified **verbatim** against the W3C spec (a summarizing fetch produced a self-contradiction mid-research; only quote-don't-interpret requests were reliable, cross-checked against `graph_owl_reasoning`'s own already-shipped, spec-cited rule set). `Catalog::detect_ontology_profiles` fetches each profile's TBox straight from flakes and returns one verdict per profile, never collapsed to a single answer — an ontology can be RL-and-QL, none, or all three.
  **A real correctness bug found and fixed while writing this slice's own tests, not after**: a cardinality restriction (`owl:maxCardinality` etc.) is *always* an anonymous restriction node reached from the named class via `rdfs:subClassOf` — never a predicate asserted directly on the class. The first-written RL check (and, found by the same bug, the QL check the `fix(99)` commit had just added) treated the restriction's own subject as the offending class, which happens to work for a hand-built fixture but is wrong for real RDF/OWL. Fixed by walking `subclass_of` into the restriction set, mirroring the pattern `graph_owl_reasoning_el::find_forbidden_axioms` already used correctly since Epic 98.
- [x] **Out-of-profile reasoning refused, naming the first offending axiom** *(5 August 2026)*. `route_ontology_reasoning` prefers RL, then EL, then QL (decision 5); an ontology in none of the three returns `Refused { first_offending_axiom, reason }`.
- [x] **Override marks the result partial, carrying what was ignored** *(5 August 2026)*. `Catalog::force_ontology_reasoning(profile)` — the `force` flag deliberately placed at the caller (`Catalog`), not in the pure grammar-check crate, since accepting a partial result is a policy decision. Returns `PartialRouting { profile, ignored }`.
- [x] **Detection over 400k axioms completes in seconds** *(5 August 2026)* — a syntactic construct-presence scan, not a reasoning pass; measured, not assumed, the same discipline Epic 98's 100k-class run used.
- [ ] **Profile badge + the reasoner that produced each derivation** *(UI → Epic 41 Slice G)*. Epics 98 and 99 add reasoners with **different completeness guarantees**, so an unlabelled conclusion is one whose strength cannot be assessed
- [ ] **Out-of-profile and override-partial results marked**, not by colour alone
- **Scope decision, recorded rather than silently narrowed**: the sub/super-class *positional* asymmetry (e.g. RL's `AllValuesFrom` legal as a superclass restriction, illegal in a subclass's own definition) is not checked — this pass checks construct presence, the same shape Epics 98/99 already established, not a full grammar parse. Conservative in one direction only: a flagged construct is always genuinely out-of-profile.
- [x] **Routing is now actually enforced on the live reasoning path — found and fixed 8 August 2026** (`plans/EPIC-COMPLETION-PLAN.md` Phase 1.4). `detect_ontology_profiles`/`route_ontology_reasoning`/`force_ontology_reasoning` were correct and tested, but `POST /reasoning/runs` never called any of them — an ontology outside every profile loaded into the RL engine silently produced a wrong hierarchy through the real API, the exact failure this epic exists to prevent. Fixed: the endpoint now refuses (naming the first offending axiom, or which profile routing prefers instead of RL) unless `?force=true`, in which case it proceeds and marks the response `partial: true` with `ignoredAxioms` naming exactly what RL's own check found wrong. Also added `GET /ontology/profile` — asking "what profile is this?" previously required attempting a run and reading the refusal. **A second, independent predicate-registry gap found the same way Phase 1.1 found `sh:sparql`/`sh:select` missing**: twelve OWL constructs the RL/EL/QL *detectors* themselves read (`disjointUnionOf`, `hasKey`, `propertyChainAxiom`, `allValuesFrom`, `unionOf`, `complementOf`, and all six cardinality predicates) were never added to the predicate registry at all — V4 seeded only the six predicates the original eight RL *rules* read, never the broader vocabulary the profile *detectors* added later need. A real Postgres store refused to assert any of them; invisible because `graph_owl_api`'s own unit tests use an in-memory fake that does not enforce registration. Fixed with V11. Proven end to end in `crates/graph-owl-server/tests/reasoning.rs`'s new `profile_routing` module — including the negative control (a plain `subClassOf` hierarchy is unaffected) and the genuine three-way-refused case, not just the two-way "routes to EL instead" case a first attempt at the RED test actually produced.

### Epic 101 — SPARQL federation
- [x] **An unlisted `SERVICE` endpoint is refused by name** *(Slice A, 5 August 2026)*. The single most important safety property in this epic landed before any capability that could leak data exists to misuse: `graph-owl-api`'s new `federation` module implements `spareval::DefaultServiceHandler` (the executor is adopted — `spargebra` already parses `SERVICE`, `spareval` already takes the handler — this slice is one trait implementation), checked against a `Catalog`-level allow-list (`Catalog::with_federation_endpoints`) that is **empty by default**, the safe direction: a deployment that never configures federation gets none, matching every other off-by-default capability in this facade. An allow-listed endpoint reaches the handler rather than being refused for the allow-list reason, proven by the placeholder error changing shape — real federation is Slice B.
  **Found a real, pre-existing bug while writing this slice's own RED test**, unrelated to federation itself: `execute_algebra`'s `collect()` silently discarded any per-row evaluation error via `filter_map(Result::ok)`. Nothing before `SERVICE` could produce a row-level error (only query-level ones, already handled), so the gap was invisible — a federation call refused by the handler was surfacing as a **successful query with zero rows**, exactly the "confidently wrong" failure mode this project refuses everywhere else. Fixed to `collect::<Result<Vec<_>, _>>()`, short-circuiting on the first row error the same way a query-level error already does.
- [x] **`SERVICE` against an allow-listed endpoint joins correctly, bounded by a timeout** *(Slice B, 5 August 2026)*. `FederationServiceHandler::handle` serializes the clause's own pattern (`SELECT * WHERE { pattern }`), calls the endpoint via `reqwest` (bridged from the synchronous trait method through `Handle::block_on`, safe only because the whole evaluation now runs inside `spawn_blocking` — `execute_algebra` did not do this before federation and needed it added, mirroring `cypher_stream`'s existing use of the same pattern), and parses `application/sparql-results+json` via `sparesults`, converting straight into `spareval`'s own `QuerySolutionIter` via its `From<ReaderSolutionsParser<R>>` impl. Proven against a real local axum SPARQL endpoint (not a stub): a joined query's rows include the mock's data, and a server that never answers fails at its configured timeout (100ms) rather than waiting out a 5s server-side delay. `cargo mutants` on `federation.rs`: 8 caught, 1 unviable, 0 missed.
- [x] **`SILENT` is honoured; endpoints that answered or failed are named** *(Slice C, 5 August 2026)*. `spareval` already swallows a `SILENT` clause's failure unprompted — `handle` is never even told which clauses are silent — so what this slice adds is visibility: `FederationServiceHandler` logs every call's endpoint and outcome, and `Catalog::execute_algebra` reads that log back only after evaluation succeeds, at which point any recorded failure can only have been silenced (a non-silent one would have propagated). `SparqlOutcome` gains `federated_endpoints`/`silenced_endpoints`, surfaced over HTTP as `federatedEndpoints`/`silencedFailures`.
  **Scope correction, found while implementing:** the plan asked for *per-row* endpoint attribution. Traced through `spareval` 0.2.6's own evaluation (`encode_bindings` → `put_variable_value`): a `DefaultServiceHandler`'s returned bindings are filtered down to the `SERVICE` clause's own statically-known variables before being merged into a row — there is no hook to smuggle a marker binding through to the final result, and no other exists. Shipped **query-level** attribution instead — which endpoints the query touched, not which row each came from. Full reasoning is in `plans/101-sparql-federation.md`'s Slice C entry.
- [x] **Bindings denied by policy never transmitted** *(Slice D, 5 August 2026 — "the important test in this epic")*. Proven by capturing the literal outbound HTTP request (a real local axum endpoint that records its own query string) rather than asserting on the result: two principals, one denied an asset, run the identical query joining that asset's local data against a `SERVICE` clause. Both captured requests are **byte-identical**, and neither contains the denied value — not because `FederationServiceHandler` filters anything, but because `spareval` hands `handle` only the clause's own static pattern text and never the outer bindings a join has accumulated (`eval.rs`'s `evaluate_service` builds the outbound pattern once, before `from` is known, and merges the *response* back in afterward). The test passed on the first run: the architecture already had this property, and this is the regression guard that proves it, not a new filter.
- [x] **Remote endpoints named at the result level, `SILENT` failures visible, allow-list admin with dry-run** *(Slice E, 5 August 2026)*. Per Slice C's scope correction, attribution is result-level, not per-row: the Workbench shows a `federated:` tag per endpoint that answered, and a warning naming every `SERVICE SILENT` endpoint that failed — an empty-looking table region no longer reads as "no such data". A new Admin → Federation tab lists the configured allow-list and a dry-run ("would this endpoint be allowed") checked client-side against the loaded list.
  **Scope correction:** the plan asked for allow-list add/remove from Admin. Unlike a policy, the allow-list has no persisted store behind it — it is set once at startup from `GRAPH_OWL_FEDERATION_ENDPOINTS` (newly wired in `main.rs`), not read fresh per request the way `Storage`'s policies are. A write UI would either do nothing past the next restart or require building that persisted store first, which is real future work this slice does not imply. The panel is read-only plus a client-side dry-run for exactly that reason.
  **Live-verified with `agent-browser`**: `graph-owl-server` run open (no auth) against a throwaway Postgres, `GRAPH_OWL_FEDERATION_ENDPOINTS` naming a live local mock SPARQL server plus a dead port. A federated query showed the `federated:` tag with the correct endpoint; a `SERVICE SILENT` query against the dead port showed the SILENT warning and no federated tag; Admin → Federation listed both configured endpoints; the dry-run correctly reported "Allowed" for the live endpoint and "Not allowed" for `https://dbpedia.org/sparql`. Zero console errors.

### Epic 102 — Read/write partition split — **shipped 8 August 2026, built on explicit override**
- [x] **The split itself.** `flakes` (the original table) renamed to `flakes_main` — zero data moved, zero index rebuilt, all four original index orderings kept under their original names — and a new `flakes_delta` table added with the same columns and exactly one index (the same SPOT-style uniqueness `flakes_main` already had, for the same write idempotency). A `flakes` **view** unions both, named columns rather than `SELECT *` after that exact mistake failed the migration outright once (`flakes_main`'s physical column order, shaped by an earlier `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN`, doesn't match a fresh `CREATE TABLE`'s logical order — `UNION types integer and text cannot be matched`). `write()` now targets `flakes_delta` explicitly, the one and only place a row is written, unchanged as an invariant. `current_state_query` and `push_live_flakes` — the query builders behind `query_pattern`/`count`/`explain`/the whole traversal engine — needed **zero code changes**: pointing `FROM flakes` at a view spanning both partitions makes current-state resolution span them too, for free. `compact(batch_size)` moves rows oldest-first via one atomic `DELETE ... RETURNING` feeding an `INSERT ... SELECT` CTE — no explicit transaction to get wrong, and repeated small-batch calls are what make compaction interruptible: every call either fully happens or fully does not, and the view resolves correctly regardless of which side a row currently sits on. 10 correctness tests (`tests/partition_split.rs`) cover every plan AC: identical query results before/after compaction, a retraction in delta superseding an assertion already compacted into main, `as_of` unaffected by a compaction boundary falling inside the queried history, and — checked *between* every single-row batch, not only at the end — a consistent view at every point of a multi-batch compaction. Two pre-existing tests needed real adaptation, not just renaming: `index_orderings.rs` (Epic 4's four-index test) now seeds `flakes_main` by hand, since `assert_flakes` alone leaves it empty pre-compaction; and its blanket "no sequential scan" check was narrowed to `flakes_main` specifically after it correctly flagged a `Seq Scan on flakes_delta` that is the entire tradeoff the split makes (delta intentionally carries one index), not a regression.
  **Built on an explicit override — the plan's own entry condition (Epic 37a Slice C's write-throughput measurement) had not fired** (flat 53,641-57,919 flakes/s across 1M-10M synthetic flakes, no observed crossover under the default 128MB `shared_buffers`). Decision 5's own measurement gate was treated as live rather than a formality: at real 1M-flake scale, write throughput measured **71,941 flakes/s — 25-34% faster** than Slice C's baseline (one index to maintain instead of four, the direction the design predicts). Read latency for the same pattern query Slice C benchmarked was **28.26ms before compaction, 32.46ms after** — both dominated by the *same* sequential-scan planner behaviour Slice C already documented for this low-selectivity query, not a new cost the union introduces; the 4ms delta traced via `EXPLAIN (BUFFERS)` to a cold cache right after compaction's own I/O, not a structural regression, and both numbers sit in the same order of magnitude as Slice C's own 23ms at the identical checkpoint. Decision 5's bar ("if it costs more on reads than compaction saves on writes, the split is a loss") was not met on this measurement, so the split shipped — a reasoned, single-run judgement at one scale, recorded with the same hedged confidence Slice C itself used for a comparably-shaped finding, not overclaimed as definitive.
- [x] **Compaction trigger + partition-health metric — found and fixed 8 August 2026** (`plans/EPIC-COMPLETION-PLAN.md` Phase 1.5/1.6). `compact()` existed and was correctly tested (`tests/partition_split.rs`), but had zero callers outside its own tests — no `Catalog` wrapper, no route, no scheduled task. In a real deployment `flakes_delta` grew forever and the whole point of the split degraded to nothing. Also confirmed the `(UI → Epic 41 Slice G)` annotation below was optimistic: there was no backend metric for a UI to consume at all, not merely an unbuilt panel. Fixed: `TripleStore::compact`/`partition_health` are now trait methods (default no-op/`None` for a backend with no partition split, so nothing else implementing the trait needed to change), `Catalog::compact`/`partition_health` wrap them, and `POST /admin/compact` (manual-trigger only — automatic scheduling is a separate, larger design question, deliberately not attempted) plus `GET /admin/partition-health` (`deltaRows`, `oldestDeltaT`) expose them over HTTP, following the existing `/admin/export`/`/admin/restore` admin-tier convention (undocumented in the OpenAPI contract, matching every other `/admin/*` route). Proven end to end in `crates/graph-owl-server/tests/partition_admin.rs`: a fresh write is visible in the backlog, compaction empties it and reports how many rows moved, and compacting an empty delta is a real no-op rather than an error.
- [x] A partition-health panel in admin — **shipped 8 August 2026**. `PartitionHealthPanel.tsx`, a new "Partition health" admin tab (`Statistic` display over `deltaRows`/`oldestDeltaT`, plus a `POST /admin/compact` "Compact now" button following `BoltSessionsPanel.tsx`'s own read-only-panel precedent). `deltaRows`/`oldestDeltaT` both `null` — no partition split — renders as a real, legitimate info state, not an error. `oldestDeltaT` shown as the raw transaction time `t`, not a fabricated "age": `partition_health`'s own DTO carries no wall-clock companion to convert it with. Verified end to end against the real demo dataset on a spare port: 1,319 delta rows before, clicked Compact, 319 after with "Moved 1000 rows into the main partition." and the oldest transaction advancing 1 → 101.

### Epic 103 — In-process traversal — **built and measured, 8 August 2026, still not routed in production**
- [x] The traversal path — `graph-owl-traversal-memory::InMemoryTraversalEngine`, a second `TraversalEngine` implementation over petgraph. Its differential suite (26 tests ported verbatim from the Postgres CTE adapter's own, plus 10 more closing gaps mutation testing found) passes unchanged against both adapters; `cargo mutants` 0 missed. A real gap found and fixed along the way: `all_paths(x, x, ...)` had no trivial zero-length self-path, unlike the Postgres frontier's own depth-0 base case
- [x] Authorization holds through the real adapter, not just a mock — `graph-owl-api`'s new `the_real_in_memory_adapter_still_respects_the_access_predicate_per_principal` plugs the real `InMemoryTraversalEngine` into `Catalog::walk_hop`'s authorization seam and runs the same query as two principals
- [x] **Measured, not assumed**: Postgres CTE beats the in-memory adapter at every depth tested, on trees up to 88,572 edges, including full-tree walks — the in-memory adapter's fixed per-call extraction cost (one full-table fetch, since decision 2 refuses a warm graph) never gets amortized away within the tested range. No crossover found, so no routing threshold could be derived — the honest answer, not the wanted one
- [x] **A real, independent production bug found and fixed getting to that number**: `graph-owl-engine-postgres`'s `push_logical_edges` (Epic 7a) had a three-way self-join whose `relType` lookup the planner mis-costed into a nested loop — 19,680 loops over a 39,360-row CTE, 31.8s for a two-hop walk over under 10,000 edges. Rewritten as one `FILTER`-aggregate pass; 49.6ms for the identical answer, 641× faster, all 28 pre-existing Postgres traversal tests (plus two new ones for `derived` and the `'related'` default, previously untested on the Postgres side) passing unchanged
- [x] **No UI — by design, verified 8 August 2026.** Which `TraversalEngine` adapter answers a walk (Postgres CTE vs. in-process petgraph) is an internal routing decision with no observable difference in results — same query, same authorization, same shape, only latency differs. `103-in-process-traversal.md` names no console/UI acceptance criterion anywhere in its own text

### Epic 104 — Ontology alignment — **shipped, 9 August 2026**
- [x] **A** The alignment fact and its store — `Alignment::Match`/`EquivalentClass`, stored as flakes carrying the real `skos:`/`owl:` predicate plus a reified metadata node. Decision 3's refusal (a computed source can never assert `owl:equivalentClass`) is structural: `AssertableSource` has no `Computed` variant, pinned by a `compile_fail` doctest
- [x] **B** UMLS RRF ingestion, resumable — `graph_owl_connectors::umls` parses real `MRCONSO.RRF` text (verified against the UMLS Reference Manual). Resumable by construction: every row maps to its own self-contained alignment, so "skip N, continue" and "process from the start" produce the identical union once every row has been seen once. **Delivery mechanism shipped 8 August 2026** (`plans/EPIC-COMPLETION-PLAN.md` Phase 3 item 3.13, decision 4.8): `ingest_mrconso` had no caller anywhere until now — `graph-owl umls-ingest --in <file.RRF> [--skip N]`, a new CLI subcommand, streams the file into the catalog one `POST /alignments` per qualifying row and reports `resume with --skip N` on completion, matching `20-metadata-as-code.md`'s own "streaming a multi-gigabyte file is a CLI-shaped operation" boundary alongside `backup`/`restore`. One alignment per HTTP call, deliberately not a new bulk endpoint: `upsert_alignment` already refuses to let an automated write overwrite a human-confirmed alignment, and a bulk fast path would have bypassed that guarantee. `graph-owl-connectors` was **not** added as a CLI dependency — it unconditionally pulls in `sqlx`/`tokio`/`rdkafka`/`pulsar` for its other connector modules, which would have broken this crate's own documented "thin client, minimal deps" rule, so the ~30-line MRCONSO parser is independently duplicated in the CLI, verified against the identical Reference Manual fixture. `graph_owl_connectors::umls` itself gained `mrconso_alignments` (raw `Alignment`s, no local graph required) with `ingest_mrconso` rebuilt on top of it — zero behaviour change, proven by a regression test. Mutation testing on the CLI's first pass found a real gap — the `--skip` resume boundary (`<=` vs `>`) had no coverage independent of a live server — closed by extracting `already_processed`/`one_based_line_number` as their own tested functions; `ingest()`'s own HTTP-calling body still has no direct test, matching `backup()`/`restore()`'s own already-accepted gap in this crate, not a new one.
- [x] **C** Cross-vocabulary traversal — SNOMED reaches its `RxNorm` counterpart through a shared CUI via a real `Catalog::sparql` join, no computed matcher involved; a lossy-reverse mapping is distinguishable by an ordinary query reading `dsc:lossyReverse` off the reified node. **Found and fixed three real bugs to get here**: `scope_facts` had no notion of vocabulary content (a CUI is not a catalog asset, so it was silently filtered out of every query); `Alignment::subject()` produced a syntactically invalid IRI (embedding real IRIs, each with their own `#`, inside another IRI); and `scoped_facts`'s own flake dedup — a latent bug in the shared query path, not alignment-specific — missed duplicates separated by a different flake with the same sort key, which two overlapping pushdown scans on one predicate produce routinely
- [x] **D** Computed alignment, confirmation, and the review queue — `graph_owl_core::extraction::Disposition` reused directly for decision 4's confidence bands (`00c`'s generic `0.8`/`0.5`, not Epic 17's own `0.9`/`0.6` `graph_owl_resolution::bands`, which looked like the obvious fit by name and was checked and rejected). `Catalog::upsert_alignment` retracts-then-asserts (mirroring `run_reasoning`'s own pattern) and refuses to let an automated run overwrite a human-confirmed alignment, while still allowing a second human call through. `Catalog::pending_alignment_review` lists the `Surface`-band entries a review-queue UI would read
- [x] **`POST /alignments` and `GET /alignments/review` — found and fixed 8 August 2026** (`plans/EPIC-COMPLETION-PLAN.md` Phase 1.7). `Catalog::upsert_alignment`/`pending_alignment_review` had no HTTP route at all — nothing could write a curated or human-confirmed alignment, or read the review queue, over the real API. Fixed: `POST /alignments` (admin-gated, the same tier as `/reasoning/runs` — an `owl:equivalentClass` write is exactly the class of operation a reasoner draws conclusions from) accepts `kind: "match"|"equivalentClass"`, refusing a `computed` source against `equivalentClass` at the request boundary (decision 3, naming `source.kind` rather than surfacing as an opaque type-conversion failure the way the compile-time-only `AssertableSource` enforcement would for a direct caller). `GET /alignments/review` (read-only, not admin-gated) resolves the raw `confidence` flakes `pending_alignment_review` returns into the properties a reviewer actually needs (`Catalog::pending_alignment_review_detailed`, a new method) — left, right, source kind/detail, confidence, directionality — one query per pending alignment, deliberately not batched: this is a review queue, bounded by how much is actually pending, not a bulk table. **A third, independent predicate-registry gap found the same way — `graph_owl_ontology::alignment` never registered a single one of its own predicates** (`alignmentLeft`, `alignmentRight`, `alignmentSourceKind`, `alignmentSourceDetail`, `lossyReverse`, plus the direct-triple predicates `owl:equivalentClass`/`skos:exactMatch`/`closeMatch`/`broadMatch`/`narrowMatch`) — invisible because `graph_owl_api`'s own unit tests use an in-memory fake that never enforces registration. Fixed with V12. Proven end to end in `crates/graph-owl-server/tests/alignments.rs`: a curated match above the review band is written and absent from the queue, a computed match in the band lands in the queue fully resolved, decision 3's refusal fires with the right field name, and a curated `equivalentClass` is accepted. **`AlignmentReviewEntry` gained a `predicate` field, found and fixed 8 August 2026** closing the console review queue below: without it, a reviewer confirming an entry had no way to know which `skos:*Match`/`owl:equivalentClass` to re-assert (`Alignment::parts()` already writes the literal string `"equivalentClass"` for that variant, so no separate `kind` field was needed) — `graph_owl_ontology::alignment::metadata_flakes` now writes a new `alignmentPredicate` flake (migration `V13`), threaded through `pending_alignment_review_detailed` and the HTTP handler's JSON, mutation-tested clean on both diffs.
- [x] **Console**: alignment review queue — **shipped 8 August 2026** (`plans/EPIC-COMPLETION-PLAN.md` Phase 3 item 3.14, decision 4.7). Full accounting under Epic 42's "One review queue over five proposal sources" bullet, Demo 7
- [x] **Console**: the alignment that made a cross-vocabulary result reachable is inspectable — **shipped 9 August 2026** (Workbench, Epic 41). `Catalog::alignments_touched` reports, at query level (the same simplification Epic 101 Slice C's federation attribution already made — `spareval` gives no hook to tag a bound row with the flake that produced it), every alignment the query's scoped facts crossed, resolved via a new `graph_owl_ontology::alignment::alignment_subject_for(left, predicate, right)` — `Alignment::subject()`'s own formula, exposed for a caller that has only read a direct triple off the graph. `SparqlOutcome::alignments_used: Vec<AlignmentReviewEntry>` reuses Phase 3 item 3.14's exact DTO rather than a second shape; wired onto `/sparql` and `/cypher`'s shared `query_outcome_json` as `alignmentsUsed`. **Console**: `ui/src/workbench/results.ts` gained `alignmentBadgeLabel` — the acceptance criterion's own words, "not by colour alone", land in what the tag's *text* says (`exactMatch · curated`, `closeMatch · computed 62%`, confidence always printed for a computed match and never for a curated one, which is definitionally `1.0` and would just be noise repeated on every row) — with a tooltip per tag naming the alignment's own left/right terms, source detail and directionality for genuine inspectability, not merely a badge. Mutation testing on the `graph-owl-api` diff first found 7 real MISSED mutants: four in the shared metadata-resolving helper (the RED test asserted only the fields both fixture alignments happened to share, never their distinct `left`/`right`/`lossyReverse`), `pending_alignment_review_detailed` collapsing to `Ok(vec![])` invisibly under `--lib` scoping (no unit test called it directly — every existing test reached it only through the real-Postgres HTTP test), and a **new instance** of this epic's own already-once-fixed "`TriplePattern` field silently dropped" class, this time on `alignments_touched`'s own follow-up metadata lookup (`s` and `as_of`) — closed by `as_of_bounds_the_metadata_lookup_not_just_the_initial_scan`, which writes a direct triple at `t=1` and its metadata only at `t=2` and proves an `as_of=1` query reports no alignment rather than wrongly finding the later metadata. Re-run: 18 mutants, 0 missed. HTTP, end to end against real Postgres: `crates/graph-owl-server/tests/sparql_alignments_used.rs`

### Plan 111 — Engine → API → UI: the capabilities that stop at the second arrow (Slices A–B shipped 14 August 2026)

- [x] **A — path finding, engine to screen.** `TraversalEngine::shortest_path` and `all_paths` have been implemented and integration-tested in `graph-owl-engine-postgres` since Epic 7a, and a grep across `crates/` found **no facade method, no route, no MCP tool and no console caller** for either. Plan 110 named that defect for routes — *a capability that only one caller can reach is not a capability* — and this is the same defect one layer down, where the caller count was zero. Now: `Catalog::find_paths` (both endpoints authorized before the walk, through the same `authorization_key` every other graph-flake check uses, because a real asset's graph identity is a UUID and its FQN lives as a `dsc:fqn` property — checking a prefix policy against the subject id fails *open*); `POST /graph/paths`; and a **How is this connected?** panel inside the asset's Graph tab. Domain-neutral by construction: two identities, a direction, two bounds and an optional list of edge names the *caller* supplies — GST asks *invoice → supplier → filing*, a healthcare pack asks *patient → encounter → medication*, and nothing in the mechanism can tell which it is looking at. **Not connected is `200` with an empty set, never `404`**: it is the commonest true answer to the question, and the trait's own `Ok(None)` already took that position. `404` is reserved for an endpoint the principal may not see, where it is deliberately indistinguishable from one that does not exist.
- [x] **A — the mutation run found the one thing six tests missed.** Five of six mutants were caught; the survivor deleted the `as_of` arm of `find_paths`, because every test asked only about *now*. That is the dangerous direction for a time-travelling walk — a route reported as of July that only came into existence in August is a fact about the present wearing a date from the past. Killed by a fixture that asserts the two hops at `t=1` and `t=100` and walks at `50`, with the same question about now as its negative half so the assertion cannot pass on a walk that finds nothing ever.
- [x] **B — ask a question of the past, and ask it in either language.** `POST /cypher` had **no console caller whatsoever** — a property-graph query language this product implements end to end, reachable only with curl. And `POST /sparql` has accepted `asOf` since Epic 4 while `api.sparql()` took one argument, so the surface where re-running a question against the past is the obvious thing to want could only ask about now. The Workbench now has a SPARQL/Cypher toggle (persisted as `?lang=`, per-language drafts so switching does not silently discard work, results cleared on switch because a result answered in the other language beside a query in this one is attributed to the wrong text) and honours the global clock, with a banner naming the instant — historical data and stale data are indistinguishable on screen unless something says which this is.
- [x] **B — the assessment that prompted this was checked rather than believed, and was half wrong.** It called the temporal UI "one of the most important missing UI capabilities". The console already had `TimeControl`, `?asOf=` deep links, a time-travelling asset detail and a `GraphExplorer` that compares two instants through `graph/diff.ts`. Plan 111 records the correction rather than re-proposing shipped work — as it does for OWL EL, data-quality tests, metrics, the governance queues, analytics, the resolution queue, drift, lineage and alignment review, every one of which the assessment listed as a gap and every one of which had already shipped.

- [x] **D — the pack's blocking strategies get run, and running them found three defects.** The gap was worse than `plans/109` recorded: it was not that reconciliation declined to call blocking, it was that `graph_owl_core::blocking_strategy` — **963 lines and 38 tests of domain-neutral blocking** — had *no callers anywhere in the workspace*. Both shipped packs have declared `[[matching.blocking]]` since Epic 105 and nothing read it. Now: `Strategy` deserializes at all (its own doc comment has promised "the configuration *is* the strategy" since Epic 105 DN-2 and it carried no derive); `pack_install::read_blocking_strategies` reads the declaration at request time as `read_console_config` reads `[console]`; `Catalog::blocking_candidates` keys the subject and every candidate, bounded and reporting truncation, and says **which** strategy agreed; and `POST /packs/{pack}/candidates` resolves the pack's prefix in the handler so the facade only ever sees `1024:partyName` and cannot name a domain even by accident. Proven end to end against a **pack invented for the test** — its own prefix, namespace and field names, none of them GST's.
- [x] **D — the three defects, each invisible until the code ran.** (1) `rename_all = "snake_case"` derives `n_gram`; both packs write `ngram`, and the packs are the contract, so the wire name is pinned explicitly — caught by a round-trip test written against a real pack's shape rather than a convenient one. (2) **`NGram::key` cannot find a transposition and its doc comment said it could**: the key joins the whole sorted window set, so any changed window changes the key and a transposed identifier never blocks with its own correction — the exact case the variant exists for. `Strategy::keys` is the fix, indexing under each window, which is what n-gram blocking means. (3) The test that appeared to prove ordering-insensitivity was `key(x) == key(x)` on one record — true of every function, and the reason (2) survived from Epic 105 to now. A fourth was caught by the HTTP test asserting the import's response *body*: `POST /graph/import/rdf` reports per-subject rejections inside a `200`, so checking only the status let the test run against an empty graph and read "no candidates" as a pass.
- [ ] **D — still open, stated rather than implied.** `reconcile_pack` does not yet consult candidates when a rule reports an absence. The capability is reachable and proven; wiring it into the finding pipeline changes what the product asserts about a taxpayer's money and wants its own slice.

- [x] **E — a pack declares its own upload surfaces.** `[console.reconciliation]` and `[[console.queues]]` had already moved; the *import* surfaces never had. `packSurfaces.ts` held a `REGISTRY` constant with GST's file list — keys, labels, and the sentence telling a user where to download each file — so a second pack's uploads needed a React change, which is exactly the test this plan applies to itself. `[[console.imports]]` now carries all of it, the console prefers the pack's declaration and falls back to the registry for a pack installed before it declared its files. **The honest boundary, stated rather than discovered later: a parser is code.** A pack cannot declare a CSV reader in TOML, so `format` selects a reader the console implements — and **a format this console has no reader for is named on screen rather than silently dropped**, because a surface that quietly disappears looks like a pack that forgot to declare it when the truth is usually that the console build is older than the pack it serves.
- [x] **E — the guard is worth more than the feature.** `read_console_config` camelCases the whole table, so `how_to_obtain` must arrive as `howToObtain`; a test reads the **shipped** pack and asserts it does. That exact failure has happened here once — `match_key` reached the console under a name nothing read, and the reconciliation silently fell back to matching on the printed identity, which looks like a working page. Stryker on the new module: 67.65% → 74.51%, with the remaining survivor in this code an equivalent mutant (`["Stryker was here"]` for `[]` — a string has no `.format`, so no reader matches and the answer is still empty), recorded rather than chased.

- [x] **F — the pack's blocking candidates reach the reviewer, closing the one item Plan 111 left open.** Slice D made the strategies runnable and nothing in the finding pipeline consulted them. They are now computed when a finding is **opened**, not attached when it is **recorded**: attaching at reconcile time would bake a judgement into stored evidence, re-run a scan per finding written rather than per finding read, and go stale the moment the next import lands. `Catalog::finding_subject` returns the subject as a *resolved* `Sid` and the pack that raised it — a key computed against an identity the graph cannot resolve matches nothing and reads as a clean result, so an unresolvable namespace is `None` rather than a fabricated id. `surviving_candidates` drops anything the walk already drew (a node listed twice tells a reviewer there is a second record when there is one) and the near miss (already on screen carrying a *stronger* claim; the same record at two strengths teaches a reviewer to trust neither). Two caps, both stated: 1,000 scanned, 5 shown.
- [x] **F — `candidates` is a separate wire field from `nearMiss`, deliberately.** They are different claims and this plan has refused to flatten strengths of evidence at every step: a near miss means *the rule declared a similarity band and a value matched exactly*; a candidate means *a blocking key collided*. Each candidate carries `by` — which strategies agreed — because "an n-gram key collided" and "a normalized key collided" change what a reviewer does next. **Console**: a "Might be the same record" section in the findings queue's evidence panel, strategy tags first, since the tag is what says how much weight the row carries and burying it after the provenance would make every candidate read alike. An absent field reads as an empty list, so a console older than its server renders rather than fails.
- [x] **F — and running it found the shipped GST pack was misconfigured.** **Every strategy the pack declared keyed a *supplier*, and every finding it raises is about an *invoice*** — so blocking could never fire on a finding however well the machinery worked. Found by wiring the candidates into the panel and getting an empty list for a scenario that plainly has a near-miss in it. The pack now declares an n-gram over `gst:invoiceNumber`: the reconciliation's own join already matches identical numbers, and what no exact join can see is a *mistyped* one. **That is the third time in Plan 111 that running a capability for the first time found the declaration around it wrong** — after `ngram`/`n_gram` and after `NGram::key`'s doc comment. A capability nobody calls is not merely unused; the configuration and documentation around it decay unobserved.

- [x] **A — the authorization design was wrong, and only real data showed it.** `POST /graph/paths` answered `404` for **every** subject on a live deployment, including subjects the same principal reached through `/packs/{pack}/candidates` in the same breath. Two layers. (1) A **pack-vocabulary subject was judged by a policy about asset names**: `authorization_key` falls back to the bare subject id when there is no `dsc:fqn`, a GST invoice has none, and no `FqnPrefix` rule matches `pr-INV-1012` — so the check failed *closed* for all pack data, the exact inverse of the fail-*open* trap this crate already documents. Every unit test missed it because they all used a policy-less `Principal::system()`, which compiles to `AccessPredicate::All`; the new test installs a real policy. (2) **A `dsc:`-namespaced UUID is not necessarily an asset** — `tables` and `assets` are different relations, so a table's id looks exactly like an asset's and has no asset row. The first fix asked `get_asset_for`, which collapses "no such asset" and "an asset you may not see" into one `NotFound` deliberately, so it cannot tell them apart, and here they need opposite answers; every table endpoint became a `404` and the integration suite caught it immediately. The shipped check reads the asset row, falls through when there is none, and otherwise compares against `fully_qualified_name`. **The guarantee is now stated narrowly rather than overstated**: pack subjects have no per-subject authorization anywhere in this system, and making this one route stricter than `graph_context`/`finding_evidence_graph`/`blocking_candidates` did not add safety, it removed the feature.
- [x] **A — verified live against the real GST estate**, not only in tests: `1024:pr-INV-1012 → 1024:supplier-27AABCU9603R1ZM` returns the route through the real supplier, a two-hop route reaches the matching GSTR-2B invoice, an unconnected pair is `200` with an empty set, and a bad direction is a `400` naming the field.
- [~] **A/B/C — the console halves are still unverified by a signed-in human.** The deployment is OIDC-only with no CLI-mintable credential, and a principal with no roles is denied by default (documented, not a defect), so row counts on the Workbench and the contradictions queue could not be confirmed end to end. The bundle is served and the routes answer; what is untested is a human clicking through them. **Named gap, per rule 3.**
- [x] **The real reason all of this looked unbuilt: the running server embedded a stale UI bundle.** It served `index-DyNnv8Y1.js` while disk held `index-D-IGoSci.js` — exactly the failure `crates/graph-owl-ui/build.rs`'s own doc comment warns about ("a rebuilt frontend is silently ignored and the binary keeps serving whatever was embedded first... the server starts, returns 200, and serves a stale bundle"). `ui/dist` is gitignored and `rust-embed` reads it at macro-expansion time, so **a UI change is not deployed until the server binary is rebuilt and the process restarted**. Nothing in the test suite can see this, and by Plan 111's own thesis a capability nobody can reach is not shipped.


### Plan 112 — Graph storage reachable from the console (Slices A–C shipped 14 August 2026)

- [x] **A — the explorer can narrow a neighbourhood to the edges a reader cares about.** `EdgeFilter.relationship_types` has existed in the traversal engine since Epic 7a and `asset_subgraph` passed `None` for it unconditionally, so the explorer had depth and time and neither of the other two controls the capability assessment asks for — a hub node's neighbourhood was complete and told the reader nothing. `SubgraphQuery` gained `relationshipTypes` (comma-separated, so a pasted URL shows the whole filter in one token), and the selection persists as `?edges=` so a filtered picture is shareable. **`Some(vec![])` means *match nothing*, not *match everything*** — a filter that silently widens to unfiltered is a control that looks like it works and does not — and the product decision that *no selection* means unfiltered lives in the console, which sends no parameter at all in that case. **The filter is carried into expansion**, because a filter applied to the seed and dropped on a click produces a picture that is half filtered and half not with nothing on screen to say which half is which. **An unknown edge name matches nothing rather than being a `400`**: there is no vocabulary to validate against, since catalog edges use `RelationshipType`'s seven names and a pack's edges carry whatever its own `relType` flakes hold — the draft plan asked for a `400` and it would have been the hardcoding the neutrality rule forbids.
- [x] **B — `PathFinder` sends the filter its own API has accepted since Plan 111.** `api.findPaths` and `POST /graph/paths` both took `relationshipTypes`; the screen sent everything except that, so "how is this connected" could not be narrowed to *how* you care about. Options are passed in from the explorer rather than fetched, so the dropdown costs no second walk.
- [x] **C — asset analytics reaches the console.** `GET /assets/{id}/analytics` and `api.assetAnalytics` both existed and `grep assetAnalytics ui/src` matched only the client definition — the third instance of the defect Plan 111 named twice. A "How connected is this?" panel: degree in/out ranked most-connected first (the reason to open it is "what is the hub here", and walk order buries the answer), orphans tagged, edge types read off the payload, and **truncation stated rather than inferred from a count** — "3 nodes" from a walk that stopped early is a claim the server never made. Ranked on in **plus** out, not their difference; a payload whose vectors disagree in length throws rather than rendering a shorter prefix that would look complete. `PageRank` deliberately absent, matching the facade.
- [x] **A–C — 100% Stryker on both new modules (85.19% → 100%), and one survivor was killed by deleting dead code** rather than by a test: `id.includes(":") ? id.slice(id.indexOf(":") + 1) : id` has provably identical arms, which is exactly why no test could tell them apart.
- [x] **Two defects the browser found that nothing else could**, past `tsc`, ESLint, 775 unit tests and a 100% mutation score. (1) **A shared filtered URL had no filter control and blamed the wrong thing**: `?edges=parentDatabase` loads with the filter already set, so no unfiltered walk ever happens in that session and an accumulator seeded from walk responses is empty — the control disappeared and the empty picture read *"Nothing is connected to this node at this depth"*, the exact claim the slice exists to avoid making. The plan's own correction was right that the options cannot come from the filtered response and still named the wrong source; they now come from the analytics payload, which walks unfiltered by construction. Found on the very link the slice made shareable. (2) **Namespace noise and raw UUIDs** — the summary read "connected by `1:parentSchema`" and every table row was a hex string while the canvas directly above had the names.
- [x] **Deployment is a gate item now, not a nicety.** `ui/dist` is gitignored and `rust-embed` reads it at macro-expansion time, so nothing is live until `npm run build` → `cargo build --release -p graph-owl-server` → restart. Plan 111 shipped three slices that were correct in the tree and invisible in the browser for exactly this reason; browser verification without that sequence verifies the previous build.
- [ ] **D — provenance explorer: scoped and deliberately deferred.** `node_sources` takes a bare `Sid` and applies **no authorization at all**, contained today only because its one caller reaches it through a finding the principal could already see. "Click any node → its source documents" would let a caller enumerate which import documents assert facts about any subject, and pack-vocabulary subjects have no per-subject authorization anywhere in this system (Plan 111 Slice A's recorded limitation). The honest version is provenance for a node an authorized walk already returned — a small extension of the evidence panel, not a new explorer. **Named gap, per rule 3.**


### Plan 113 — Graph exploration for any subject, not only catalog assets (Slices A–D shipped 14 August 2026)

- [x] **A — `graph_context` reaches HTTP, and it had zero callers before this.** `Catalog::graph_context` shipped with Epic 105 as a GraphRAG primitive that walks from any `Sid` and resolves per-node provenance — and nothing called it. **The fourth instance in two plans of a capability that stops one layer short of anything that can reach it**, after `shortest_path`, `blocking_strategy` and `assetAnalytics`. `POST /graph/context` now exposes it with the relationship filter and `asOf`, authorized per node through `graph_subject_visible`. The walk and the provenance half were split into `walk_authorized` / `assemble_graph_context` so Slice B reuses the first without re-deriving it. **`parse_node_id` gained a third accepted shape — a full IRI — and the check order is load-bearing**: `http(s)://` must be tested *before* the general `contains(':')` case, or an IRI's own `https:` colon routes it into `parse_sid`, which tries to read `"https"` as a numeric namespace code and fails naming the wrong thing.
- [x] **B — connectivity, generalized, and a real bug caught before it shipped.** `POST /graph/context/analytics` returns the **same envelope** as `/assets/{id}/analytics` deliberately, so the console's `AssetAnalytics` type and rendering are reused unchanged — degree means the same thing whether the node is a table or an invoice. Writing the RED test found that `analytics_over_subgraph` derived `edge_types` from raw flakes *independently of* the `relationship_types` filter applied to the walk: a filtered picture and its filtered numbers would have disagreed. The filter is now threaded through, and `the_filter_narrows_what_analytics_measures_too` pins it.
- [x] **C — a subject explorer, reachable from where the console already renders bare subject ids.** `SubjectExplorer` (panel), `ClickableSubject` (link owning its own `Drawer`) and `subjectContext.ts` (pure logic, **100% Stryker, 32/32 killed**). Wired into the findings queue's near-miss and candidate rows — which required carrying `iri` through `evidenceNearMiss` and `evidenceCandidates`, because those rows held only a bare local `id` that resolves to nothing. **`ConnectivityPanel` hardcoded `api.assetAnalytics`** and a GST invoice has no asset id to call it with, so it was refactored to a caller-supplied `load` + `cacheKey`; it would otherwise have been unusable for exactly the subjects this plan exists to reach. **Deliberately list-based, not a canvas** — `GraphCanvas`'s `Picture` needs catalog-shaped nodes with a `kind` a pack subject lacks, and `00f` requires the accessible equivalent anyway, so it is built first rather than after.
- [x] **D — per-entity reasoning stops assuming the catalog namespace.** `GET /reasoning/derived` parsed its subject with `parse_sid` (`namespace:local` only) while every identity the console actually holds for a pack subject is an **IRI** — so the one screen showing *what follows that nobody wrote down* could not be called with the identity the UI has. One-word swap to `parse_node_id`. The component half cost more: `ReasoningView` lived in `App.tsx`, which imports `SubjectExplorer` transitively, so importing it back would have closed a cycle — it moved to `ui/src/graph/ReasoningView.tsx`, which was risk-free precisely because **none of its dependencies lived in `App.tsx`**. A component reachable from two levels of the tree does not belong in the composition root.
- [x] **Verified in the browser against real GST data, not a synthetic fixture.** Opening a real `gst:SupplierPanMismatch` finding's `2b-INV-1001` candidate walks **37 nodes and 36 relationships**, ranks `supplier-27AABCU9603R1ZM` as the hub at 36 incoming edges, and populates the relationship filter from the subject's own real edge types. Narrowing to `type` collapses picture and connectivity **in lockstep** (2 nodes in both) — the Slice B fix, confirmed against real data rather than only against its own test.
- [x] **Two traps worth naming.** A pure module named `subjectExplorer.ts` beside a component `SubjectExplorer.tsx` **collides on a case-insensitive filesystem** and silently breaks module resolution (renamed `subjectContext.ts`). And **moving code into a new file applies lint rules the old file was grandfathered under** — eight `local/no-raw-jsx-text` errors appeared with no wording changed at all.
- [x] **`cargo mutants` produced no viable mutant for `derived_about` — all four failed to compile.** An honest result for a thin parse-delegate-serialize shell, not a coverage gap: the branching that matters lives in `parse_node_id`, which mutates cleanly under `--lib` (1 caught, 1 unviable, 0 survivors). **Where a handler has no viable mutants, the mutation question belongs to the function it delegates to.**
- [ ] **Lineage, Memory, Knowledge and Disagreements stay asset-only — stated, not overlooked.** Lineage encodes pipeline provenance between catalog assets; GST's real question ("which document said this") is already answered by `node_sources` and the evidence graph, and forcing lineage semantics onto tax documents would model the wrong relationship. Memory is human commentary keyed by a UUID foreign key — extending it to arbitrary graph subjects is a schema change, not a wiring fix, and nothing in this session's evidence says GST needs it yet. **Named gaps, per rule 3.**


### Plan 120 — graph-owl's console becomes domain-agnostic; reco-now becomes a domain investigation workspace (all 8 slices shipped 16 August 2026 — Plan 120 complete)

- [x] **A — the GST ontology now loads on reco-now startup, unblocking the Ontology Builder's "gst" selector.** `_install_graphowl_pack` calls `load_pack(..., include_documents=False)` to exclude packs/gst's demo invoice fixtures (the native reconcile engine has no per-source isolation, so loading them would put graph-owl's own demo data into every reconciliation reco-now runs) — but `include_documents=False` skips every `[[documents]]` entry indiscriminately, and `ontology.ttl` (source `gst-ontology`) is declared there too, alongside `law/sections.ttl`/`law/rule-36-4.ttl`, which the surrounding code already knew to import back individually. `ontology.ttl` just wasn't in that list, so a reco-now deployment never had the ontology loaded and the Ontology Builder read "No ontology installed yet" — not a broken pack, a startup step silently skipping a file. Verified live: SPARQL against `graph:import:gst-ontology` showed 0 triples before, 90 after.

- [x] **D — a re-upload replaces prior data for its source instead of accumulating beside it, closing the root cause of the "hardcoded-looking" totals a live user session reported.** `Catalog::import_rdf` dedupes only within one `source`'s own named graph, and reco-now's `_ingest_to_graphowl` minted a fresh random UUID suffix per upload (`f"reco-{kind}-{uuid4()...}"`) — so every upload landed in a brand-new graph, and every finding query's unbound `GRAPH ?g {}` SPARQL pattern matched all of them at once, summing every upload ever made in the session into one number. `Catalog::delete_import(source)` already existed (used internally by `save_rdf_edit`) but had no HTTP surface; the fix is a `DELETE /graph/import/rdf?source=...` route (thin wrapper, admin-gated, validated the same way the existing `POST` rejects anything that could address a graph the caller does not own) plus a stable per-kind source name (`reco-books`/`reco-gstr2b`/`reco-gstr1`) with a delete-before-import call ahead of every upload. 14/14 tests in `graph_import.rs`, 5 new (re-import after delete lands instead of skipping, deleting an unknown source is a no-op not an error, deleting one source doesn't touch another, non-admin refused, forged source name refused).
- [x] **D — verified live end to end against the real 3-file Aug 2026 sample fixture, not only the test database.** Fresh Postgres, `POST /api/upload` landed 33/26/10 triples (books/gstr2b/gstr1) and `/api/reconcile`'s stats matched the hand-derived answer key exactly (14 total, 7 matched, 2 review, 3 only_books, 2 only_gstr2b). Re-uploading the identical files a second time produced byte-for-byte identical landed counts and reconcile stats rather than doubled ones — the actual acceptance test, since a unit test against an ephemeral database cannot show accumulation across separate uploads the way a real session does. `verify-reconcile-parity.py` still green.
- [x] **C — every screen that showed a bare subject id now shows its pack-declared name instead** (`plans/121-label-resolution.md`, 4 sub-slices). Root cause: `displayTerm()` only ever stripped an IRI to its local name and never read a literal off the subject, which is why a GST Supplier rendered as `supplier-27AAAFN2938K1Z2` everywhere — the evidence-graph drawer, Explore's `SubjectExplorer`, and the findings queue's own subject column. Fix: a pack declares `[console.labels]` (one predicate per class, e.g. `Supplier = "supplierName"`), and one shared `resolve_node_label` (namespace code → owning pack via `Catalog::namespaces()`'s `declaredBy: "pack:<id>"` → that pack's `[console.labels]` entry for the subject's own `rdf:type` → the literal) is called from all three surfaces: `finding_evidence_graph` (new `label` field per node), `graph_context_route` (`SubjectExplorer`'s data source, same field), and `list_findings` (wrapped as `FindingWithSubjectLabel` — a response-only `#[serde(flatten)]` wrapper, not a new field on the `Finding` domain type itself, since resolving a label means reading `pack.toml` off disk and `graph-owl-core` is pure domain with no I/O). Client: `nodeLabel()`/`namesFromContext()`/`subjectLabelFor()` all prefer the resolved label, falling back to the old `displayTerm(iri)` behaviour when a class has no declaration — deliberately the simplest version: one predicate per class, no composition, no per-subject override, exactly as the plan's Parking Lot describes for a later slice to add if ever needed.
- [x] **C — proven against hospitality with zero Rust or TypeScript changes**, hospitality's own pack.toml header's stated acceptance criterion for exactly this kind of feature. `[console.labels] Guest = "guestSurname"` alone made the *identical, unmodified* server code resolve a real guest's surname through the real evidence-graph route — verified as genuine, not vacuous, by temporarily removing the declaration and confirming the same test then failed with `label: null`.
- [x] **C — mutation testing across all three call sites: 0 missed.** `graph-owl-api`'s `node_literal` (the literal-lookup primitive): 5/5 caught under `--lib`. Each HTTP handler's own wiring: `finding_evidence_graph` 4/4 caught + 4 unviable, `graph_context_route` 0/4 viable, `list_findings` 1/7 caught + 6 unviable — every "unviable" batch is the same documented pattern (a thin `Result<Json<T>>` shell cargo-mutants cannot even typecheck substitutions against), not a coverage gap, since the branching logic all lives in the one shared, separately-tested `resolve_node_label`/`node_literal` pair. `openapi.json` diff empty at every step — none of the three routes' responses were schema-captured before this (untyped `Json<Value>` or, for `list_findings`, a `Finding` with no `ToSchema` derive), so adding a field changed nothing utoipa could see.
- [x] **C — live verification against a real upload found the mechanism was correct and the *data* wasn't**, one layer below it. A real Supplier subject resolved `semanticType: "Supplier"` but `label` stayed `null` — `reco-now`'s `graphowl_client.py` had always asserted `gst:supplierName` on the *invoice* subject (via the generic per-row `PREDICATES` loop), never on the Supplier subject `[console.labels]` (and `ReconciliationWorkspace.tsx`'s own party-name query) both expect it on. This is very likely the underlying cause of the session's original complaint about the supplier name never showing anywhere — the resolution mechanism this plan built was never wrong, the literal it depended on simply wasn't where it looked, in code that predates this plan. Fixed by moving `supplier_name` out of `PREDICATES` and special-casing it onto the Supplier subject's own triple block, exactly matching `supplier_gstin`'s existing precedent. Confirmed live: the same real Supplier subject now resolves `label: "Nimbus Freight Logistics"`; Slice D's re-upload-holds-steady behaviour unaffected; `verify-reconcile-parity.py` still green.
- [x] **E — narrower than originally scoped, resolved by direct user direction rather than the deferred `story-splitting`/`grill-me` pass.** The plan's own open question ("what, if anything, replaces the invoice-table workflow in the console once it's gone") turned out to have a concrete answer once asked directly: keep the read-only statement/supplier/findings view, remove only the upload cards and the manual "Run reconciliation" button — reco-now's own upload flow already triggers graph-owl's native rule engine automatically (`_run_graphowl_reconcile`), so the manual trigger and the upload surface were both duplicating a step reco-now already owns, not a smaller version of a workflow graph-owl still needs to offer. `SourceCard` and its ~150 lines removed along with `running`/`packId`/`surfaces`/`unreadable` state that existed only to support it; `refresh()` (unchanged) already independently repopulates the statement/tiles/supplier view/findings from the graph on every mount, so nothing that stayed depends on anything that left. `api.reconcilePack`/`importThroughSurface` themselves untouched — both still real, still used by `PackAdminPanel.tsx` for generic pack administration.
- [x] **E — verified live**, not just by test: fresh upload through reco-now, graph-owl's console renders the statement (real ₹ figures, real supplier names throughout — benefiting from the same-day `supplierName`-on-Supplier-subject fix), the graph-knowledge tiles, the by-supplier table and the findings-by-rule breakdown, with no upload control and no run button anywhere on the page and nothing manually triggered to get there. 981/981 across the whole `ui/src` suite.
- [x] **B — the namespace-filtered graph view, restored.** Commit `5cd2fd4` (Plan 117) merged the deleted `OntologyEditor.tsx`'s Check/Save logic into `OntologyBuilder.tsx`'s Code tab without carrying its namespace filter over — a real regression, not a misremembering. Restored onto the *current* data model rather than ported literally: the old component filtered raw triples by subject/predicate namespace through Cytoscape, and the current architecture has no raw triples once imported (the model is entities/relationships, not triples), so `EntityType` gained `namespace: string | null` (populated from each class's own IRI on import; `null` for a manually added entity), and a relationship survives a namespace filter only when both its endpoints do — the closest analog to the old subject-or-predicate rule the entity/relationship split still allows. Same `namespaceOf` algorithm as the deleted code (last `#`, else last `/`). Hidden entirely when the model has no namespaced entity yet, matching the reconciliation period filter's own "a filter with nothing to filter is noise" rule. Verified live: loading gst's real ontology populates the filter with `https://graph-owl.dev/packs/gst#`, selecting it narrows the canvas without error.
- [x] **H — the standalone SPARQL/Cypher Workbench route is gone; the same component now renders as a third tab inside Ontology Builder.** Pure relocation: `WorkbenchPanel.tsx` extracts `App.tsx`'s old `WorkbenchPage` (2061-2346) with no behavior change — every helper it calls (`ui/src/workbench/*.ts`: language toggle, draft preservation, results rendering, deep-linking) is untouched, only the component using them moved. Extracting into a new file lost `App.tsx`'s grandfathered exemption from `local/no-raw-jsx-text` (a documented gotcha, hit again here), fixed with a small `COPY` object and four formatting helpers. `routes.ts` drops `"workbench"` from the CI-asserted route budget; `routes.structural.test.ts` (which greps `App.tsx`'s real section switch in both directions) proves the old route is fully gone. Verified live against the real GST-pack demo: SPARQL executed 50 rows/1988 facts read with a real query plan, Cypher executed 0 rows/0 facts read with the honest "matched nothing" message, the `lang=cypher` deep-link updated correctly, no console errors. 23 new/updated structural tests in `OntologyBuilder.structural.test.ts` prove the tab is wired (imported, mounted, receives the same `asOf`/`colors` props as the rest of the builder), not just present in source. Commit `7c0d82c`.
- [x] **F — reco-now's own navigation, copy, and theme now read as an investigation tool, not a reconciliation-results viewer.** `TopNav`'s "Reconcile" step relabelled "Investigate" (display text only — the underlying `"reconcile"` routing key in `App.jsx` untouched, keeping this framing/presentation as the plan's own scope requires); `ReconcilePage`'s "Reconciliation Results" heading relabelled "Findings", directly echoing graph-owl's own vocabulary; a "powered by GraphOWL" tag added beside the wordmark and to the browser tab title. Theme: user chose to keep the dark aesthetic over a full light/GraphOWL-palette switch, so only the accent hue changed — the green "matcha" identity (`#4ade80`, evoking a spreadsheet-matcher's checkmark) replaced by indigo (`#818cf8`). The CSS custom properties were renamed from `--color-matcha-green(-surface)` to `--color-matcha-accent(-surface)`, not just recolored — a token still named "green" after turning indigo would be actively misleading — which meant a mechanical rename of every Tailwind utility class consuming it across 10 files. Verified live against the running dev server (indigo nav pills, buttons, and upload icon; correct tab title), `npm run build` clean, no console errors. **Caught and corrected before committing**: an initial pass was mistakenly applied to `/Users/akash/KG_Projects/Reco`, a separate, non-git, non-canonical checkout on disk outside this repo — not `ext-apps/Reco`, the actual integrator this repo runs and the one the live dev server was serving from. Commit `b69c6be`.
- [x] **G — Plan 107 ("filing period as a first-class graph entity") Slice 1: `gst:FilingPeriod` exists as a real graph subject and one registered query traverses to it.** Before this, `gst:period` was a scalar literal on every fact with no node a query could start from — "what's in period X" meant filtering the literal by hand, per query. `gst:FilingPeriod` (a new class) and `gst:belongsToPeriod` (a new edge) land in `packs/gst/ontology.ttl`; `fixtures/filing-periods.ttl` declares one `FilingPeriod` instance per distinct period value already used elsewhere in the fixtures (`2020-07`/`2026-07`/`2026-08` — counted via `grep`, not invented); every existing fixture subject gets a `belongsToPeriod` triple next to its own `gst:period` literal; `queries/period-summary.sparql` (registered via `[[queries]]`) answers "everything in period X" by traversing the edge. Two decisions resolved by direct question before writing code, since `grill-me` turned out to be user-invocable only and could not be run mid-session: `gst:period` stays on every fact unchanged — the new edge is purely additive, not a replacement, so no existing rule (`GstinTransposition` binds `?period` as a literal) needed touching; and `period-summary` binds its caller-supplied period as the `FilingPeriod` subject's own IRI, not a bare period string, matching `provision-in-force.sparql`'s own precedent. **Two real gaps the RED test surfaced, not part of the original design sketch**: `pack.toml`'s `[[predicates]]` array is the actual runtime predicate registry — a property declared only in `ontology.ttl`'s own RDF triples is descriptive, not a registration, so `belongsToPeriod` needed its own `[[predicates]]` entry before any triple using it could be asserted (the pack failed to load with "24 subjects rejected" until this was added); and `placeholder_names` (the function that finds a registered query's required bindings) scans the *whole* query text, comments included — an early draft of `period-summary.sparql`'s own doc comment named `provision-in-force`'s `{{invoice}}` placeholder literally for explanatory purposes, which silently added `invoice` as a second required binding no caller could satisfy. No Rust or Python code changed — pure pack content, matching the plan's own "pack-content epic, not an engine epic" framing. Verified two ways: 3 new Rust integration tests (`crates/graph-owl-server/tests/filing_period.rs`) prove the query mechanism against synthetic-but-real-shaped data, including two manual mutations (wrong predicate, wrong `?type` binding) both killed — the second confirms a mid-session test strengthening (asserting the bound `type`, not just subject membership) was load-bearing, not cosmetic; and live against `scripts/demo.sh --gst`'s real fixtures, `period-summary` for `2020-07` returns exactly the 4 real subjects (1 `Gstr1Filing` + 1 `Gstr2bStatement` + 2 `PurchaseInvoice`), an unknown period returns an empty row set not an error, and the pre-existing 15-finding reconciliation baseline is unchanged. Commit `440c78d`.

- [x] **G, continued — Slices 2-5 shipped the same day, completing Plan 107 and, with it, Plan 120's own Slice G row.** `period-diff.sparql` (2, `df5e3bd`) generalizes `period-summary`'s traversal to two periods via a two-row `VALUES` (not `UNION`, unproven in this pack); its own RED test found asking to diff a period against itself doubled every row without `SELECT DISTINCT`. `period-history.sparql` (3, `c84ae61`) traverses a subject's own `belongsToPeriod` edges ordered by period; found live that `belongsToPeriod`'s `many = false` is enforced only within one import batch, so a genuinely multi-period subject needs two separate imports, not one. `ui/src/features/filingPeriods` (4, `da459fc`) is the console surface — a period picker plus an optional "compare against" picker calling a new `api.runPackQuery`; a real crash surfaced only by testing live in a browser (deriving table columns from the picker's own mode raced the async fetch) was fixed by deriving columns from the loaded result's own `variables` field instead. `periods-before.sparql` (5, `eb2b37a`) answers "this month vs. last" without calendar arithmetic — every period before the given one, most recent first, so "previous" means the closest period that genuinely exists rather than a computed date that might never have been filed. **A recurring bug worth naming on its own**: the exact same `placeholder_names`-scans-comments trap from Slice 1 bit `period-diff.sparql` and `period-list.sparql` too (a comment naming another query's placeholder, or describing "no placeholder" with literal double braces, silently adds a phantom required binding) — caught each time only by running the query live, until a dedicated regression test (`pack_query_hygiene.rs`, part of the Slice 4 prerequisite commit `7fc0ec7`) started catching it mechanically instead. Every slice verified live against `scripts/demo.sh --gst`'s real fixtures, not only synthetic Rust tests.
